| **[RoboArchitect: Automating the Design of Robotic Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42141v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent work shows that a frozen robot policy performs far better inside an agent harness, where control code decides when to query the policy, consult vision tools, or command the arm directly. Yet today these harnesses are built by hand and redesigned for each new robot or benchmark. We show that harness design can be automated, offering a strong alternative to finetuning on a novel setup. In RoboArchitect, a coding model observes a robot's rollouts, diagnoses failures, and iteratively revises the harness around the frozen policy. The harness evolves on a set of episodes for its optimization and is evaluated on held-out scenes and tasks. Without updating any model weights, RoboArchitect raises average success from 9.7% to 43.3% on unseen scenes (4.5x) and from 12.7% to 40.2% on unseen tasks (3.2x), across multiple benchmarks and policies. The gains are largest where the benchmark is far from the policy's training data: wrapping a general-purpose policy, the evolved harness yields a specialist that often outperforms even the finetuned version. Intuitive control strategies are automatically designed, such as lifting a jammed arm clear or reaching in horizontally when an obstacle blocks the way from above. The harness also evolves its own persistent memory, carrying learned experience, such as effective grasp depths, into later decisions. These results suggest that substantial headroom in robotic manipulation lies in the agent harness, and its design can be effectively automated and optimized without weight updates.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Website: https://roboarchitect-ai.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Metacognitive Decoding: Efficient Inference for Diffusion Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37642v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models offer a promising path to efficient agents through parallel token generation. However, efficient interaction requires adapting both where computation is spent and how much text is generated. We introduce a metacognitive controller that guides diffusion generation according to the needs of the current response and the agent's interaction with the environment. It learns when to finalize tokens and how far generation should extend, reducing overall computation within responses. Our controller, a lightweight plug-in for the diffusion backbone, is trained on agent trajectories to balance correctness and generation cost. This approach connects the agent's generation process to the interaction the agent serves. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that our approach reduces inference FLOPs by 20.6% on average compared with the most efficient baseline, while maintaining competitive task accuracy. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of our approach.</p></details> | 10 pages, 4 figures |
| **[LazyAgent: Demand-Driven Materialization and Physical Optimization of Agentic Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23058v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent runtimes that plan before acting run every step as soon as it is ready. Neither these runtimes nor their benchmarks distinguish a step that is ready from a step the request needs, and the difference is large: across 303 requests on 154 production workflows, an eager runtime that stops once the goal is met spends 62.2% of its CPU on work the request never needed. LazyAgent is, to our knowledge, the first complete formulation of demand-driven execution for agent runtimes. Its rule: run a ready step only if the current request needs it, and keep that answer exact as the plan changes. A demanded set derived from the program decides, in one traversal with no model calls, so LazyAgent spends nothing outside the request. Because demand is defined exactly, its behavior is proved: builders know before any run where demand must save and where it must tie. The bill is known before a plan runs, so LazyAgent can act as a query optimizer to choose among candidate workflows. Measured live against that eager runtime, LazyAgent saves 51.7% and 52.1% of container time on release gates where a frontier LLM writes the patches, 12.8% of tokens on DataSciBench plans at matched quality, and 24.0% of dollars inside a live LangGraph application, with no losses. When the LLM re-plans after real code failures, it solves every solvable goal with 11.7% fewer tokens per goal. On the production workflows it saves 42.0% of CPU, ties exactly where a request needs the whole graph, and adds at most 0.43% overhead. The same set steers every physical operation: projection saves 80-89% of a shared CI step, where the identical eager control saves 0.0%. Public agent benchmarks are designed the same way as eager runtimes, so they cannot show this difference. We compose our test programs from their tasks and call on industry to build benchmarks that measure how efficiently agent workflows run.</p></details> | <details><summary>57 pa...</summary><p>57 pages. v2: rewrote the abstract and introduction; added a remark on pricing plans to the appendix; results unchanged</p></details> |
| **[The Envelope Layer: An Underestimated Surface of LLM Agent Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37415v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prior work framed the envelope layer as the channel through which malicious content enters an agent, but did not examine the effects of label strength, envelope authenticity, scaffolds, or existing memories. Following its GDA Measurement and A/B/C/D adjudication, this paper takes the A2A-TIBA attack as the test vehicle and the semantic breach rate as the primary indicator, and characterizes the security boundary of the envelope layer through a 2x2 factorial experiment, a memory experiment, and control experiments (an unlabeled same-protocol baseline, a one-day interleaved control, and benign tasks). The results show that, under authentic envelopes, strong labels consistently reduce the semantic breach rate (-56 to -61 percentage points), and the benefit of weak labels is about a quarter to a half of that of strong labels; forging the envelope weakens the strong label's benefit (interaction OR = 2.10, p = 0.022) and its direction varies across frontends; the effect of memory valence exceeds that of any label manipulation; success memory raises the semantic breach rate and failure memory lowers it, while most conflict combinations return to the no-memory baseline; the interleaved control reproduces the direction that "unlabeled is worst"; and on benign tasks the cost of strong labels appears in layers across frontends and backends. Accordingly, this paper extends the envelope layer into "admission surface plus context assembly surface," whose security boundary is a dynamic outcome jointly shaped by label claims, frontend permissions, and historical memory.</p></details> |  |
| **[RAMA: Retrieval-Augmented Multi-Agent Routing with Episodic Memory for LEO Satellite Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42068v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Low-Earth orbit (LEO) satellite networks present a challenging routing environment due to rapidly changing topology, traffic, and link conditions. Existing deep reinforcement learning (DRL) routing methods typically rely on current observations and information encoded in network weights, limiting their ability to exploit successful past experiences. We propose RAMA, a retrieval-augmented multi-agent DRL framework with Approximate Information State (AIS), in which each satellite combines its local observation, recent routing history, neighboring state information, and retrieved entries from a per-satellite episodic memory. Retrieved experiences are integrated through a Transformer-based decision network trained with AIS-based auxiliary objectives. With weights frozen in a 140-satellite Kepler constellation, RAMA reduces mean end-to-end latency by 13.4% and 99th-percentile latency by 66.7% relative to the state-of-theart double deep Q-network (DDQN) routing method, mainly by avoiding long detours. Updated online, RAMA remains stable while DDQN degrades. On the 1,584-satellite Starlink shell 1, the reduction in mean latency grows to 93.2%, and updating RAMA online lowers its mean latency by a further 25.5%. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of retrieval-augmented memory for scalable decentralized routing in LEO satellite networks.</p></details> |  |
| **[REA: A Routed Embodied Agent Runtime for Asynchronous Brain-Cerebellum Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42053v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models are becoming remarkably reliable at high-level reasoning, yet they remain too slow and costly to serve as fine-grained controllers. Conversely, fast policies like VLAs and whole-body controllers offer more efficient reflexes, but struggle to generalize. While pairing a deliberative brain (foundation models) with a reactive cerebellum (fast policies) seems natural for embodied systems, two obstacles remain: methods are built and scored on incompatible stacks, and their asynchronous coordination is difficult. For example, an agent that calls the VLA in the agentic loop can stall the fast execution, while a VLA that calls the agent on errors tends to re-trigger fallbacks repeatedly or completely miss silent failures. To address this, we propose REA (Routed Embodied Agent), a unified runtime where a deliberative System 2, an embodied router System 1 and reactive System 0 policies cooperate under a single golden rule - no running policy waits on the brain. Instead of querying the brain at each failure, REA employs a route memory that stores each policy's measured success ahead of time, a boundary-triggered judge gate that consults the brain on policy execution, and a shared-budget escalation ladder for bounded recovery. Evaluated on 3,000 test episodes across 60 LIBERO-Pro tasks, REA reaches 95.9% success and beats the best single-agent baseline in its own policy pool (94.7%) while reducing control steps by 25% and control time by 17%. In a separate 100-episode RoboTwin 2.0 study, REA achieves 84% success with VLA policies and 86% with VLA+code, compared with 80% for the strongest single VLA baseline MEDex. These results demonstrate learned and programmatic policies operating within the same runtime. REA also transfers to BEHAVIOR-1K and a real Unitree G1 humanoid, achieving the highest observed success among the compared methods.</p></details> | 32 pages, 14 figures |
| **[MIRAGE: Auditing Anti-Muslim Bias in Frontier LLMs Across Reasoning, Agentic, and Time-Coupled Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16562v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Five years after the discovery of persistent anti-Muslim bias in large language models, most evaluations remain confined to single-turn prompt completion, a setting that no longer reflects how frontier LLMs are deployed. We introduce \textbf{MIRAGE} (Muslim-Identity Reasoning and Agentic Generation Evaluation), a benchmark of 1{,}200 prompts spanning three deployment-realistic conditions: direct completion, chain-of-thought reasoning, and simulated agentic decision-making across content moderation, lending triage, refugee claim summarization, and hiring screens. Across six frontier models, we find that (i) chain-of-thought reasoning \emph{amplifies} rather than suppresses Muslim-violence associations by 12--34\% relative to direct completion, (ii) agentic decisions exhibit a 9--22 percentage-point asymmetry between Muslim and matched non-Muslim cases on identical evidence, and (iii) bias is sharply time-coupled to retrieved news context, increasing 18--27\% under recent-conflict retrieval. Existing prompt-based mitigations transfer poorly across our three conditions, suppressing direct-completion bias while leaving agentic asymmetry largely intact. We release MIRAGE and an open evaluation harness to support targeted mitigation research.</p></details> | <details><summary>arXiv...</summary><p>arXiv admin note: This submission has been withdrawn because it does not meet arXiv's research content quality standards</p></details> |
| **[TradeWar: A Benchmark for Execution-Grounded Trade Negotiation with Adaptive LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42004v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn negotiation remains a challenging problem for large language model (LLM) agents. While LLMs can conduct natural-language dialogue, existing benchmarks predominantly assess only agreement rates, overlooking whether negotiated commitments actually create value once executed. To address this gap, we introduce TradeWar, a multi-country simulation benchmark that translates negotiation dialogues into concrete tariff and trade-share policies, enforces capacity and network constraints during settlement, and evaluates outcomes via an Economic Health Index (EHI). To solve this execution-grounded task, we propose TACTIC (Trade-aware Adaptive Consolidation and Theory-of-mind for International Commerce), an agent that iteratively refines its negotiation strategy across episodes-without updating base LLM weights-by leveraging prior experience, opponent behavior, and past settlement outcomes. Despite using zero-shot chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting, PromptLLM attains a 75% agreement rate but only 57.1 final EHI (*100), whereas TACTIC reaches 68.8. Across 20 sequential episodes and three seeds, TACTIC's final EHI improves from 58.2 to 73.2, compared with 65.2 for an ablation without cross-episode updates. Under technology-controls and energy-crisis shocks, TACTIC also exhibits smaller performance degradation than PromptLLM and MemoryLLM. These results indicate that CoT deliberation and interaction memory alone do not fully address delayed execution feedback, whereas settlement-grounded adaptation improves long-horizon negotiation in TradeWar.</p></details> | Accepted to DAI'26 |

Here we introduce MoLRSI, an AI agent that combines systematic optimization of workflows and model architectures with persistent cross-task memory to recursively improve its search policy for molecular discovery. We evaluate MoLRSI in two challenging molecular discovery settings that require balancing competing properties. In antibiotic discovery, MoLRSI developed a screening workflow balancing antibacterial activity, cytotoxicity and structural novelty, achieving an experimental hit rate of 15.9%, compared with 8.7% for a previous expert-led workflow using the same training data and screening library. In de novo peptide-MHC binder design, MoLRSI developed workflows that jointly consider target binding and specificity, producing eight experimentally validated hits against MART-1 and SARS-CoV-1 pMHCs with preferential binding over tested off-targets while Claude Science completely failed. These findings demonstrate how experience-guided workflow optimization can address competing discovery objectives while improving the search strategies used in subsequent campaigns.</p></details> | <details><summary>v2: u...</summary><p>v2: updated the author list</p></details> |
| **[An Agentic AI Workflow for Liver Lesion Segmentation and Structured Radiology Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34599v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite advances in automated liver lesion segmentation, a gap remains between image-derived predictions and clinically interpretable radiology reports. | **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561v5)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. | **[Concordat: Typed Sessions for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37461v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents built on large language models are increasingly allowed to take consequential actions: cancelling bookings, issuing refunds, changing accounts. We present Concordat, a Python library that treats an agent as an object whose interface is a finite-state protocol, in the spirit of typestate and session types, and enforces that protocol on the model at run time. On every turn the model is offered only the tools that are legal in the current protocol state, calls outside that set are rejected before they have side effects, decisions reserved for humans can only be requested, and token and tool-call budgets are charged and split linearly across handoffs. | **[Completeness and Cost of Retrieval Structures for Multi-Tool Requests in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41905v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An agent that must call several tools to fulfill a request fails when the tool retriever omits any one of them, yet tool retrievers are mostly built and evaluated on queries requiring a single tool. | **[META-Robot: Self-Cognition and Capability Scaling in Agentic Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41868v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based robotic agent systems can generate programs for robot control, yet their generalization to new tasks often depends on elaborate scaffolding. Simulated user assigns creative tasks to probe the director's weaknesses, while the meta engineer uses the task traces to revise the director's interaction and production strategy. Although this approach can address discovered interaction and production issues, it still relies on passive attribution by the meta agent. This limits its ability to adapt to broader user interaction patterns and production techniques. To solve this problem, we equip LIT with a co-evolution paradigm. The meta agent proactively learns external knowledge, thereby improving its ability to discover problems and propose solutions. The meta agent, simulated user, and director together form an evolutionary closed loop, collaboratively enhances the system's directing and interaction capabilities. Across the Test and OOD Test splits, LIT improves production and Interaction scores by 19.1% and 32.0%, respectively, relative to the initial pipeline. | **[SketchMate: Agentic Geometric Reasoning for Zero-Shot Precision Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41844v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inserting an unseen part requires sub-millimetre precision and the correct orientation of keyed features. | **[What Standard Evaluations Hide About Detectors of Hijacked LLM Agents: Rare Attacks, Unseen Tools and Benchmark Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41803v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Detectors can flag hijacked large language model (LLM) agents from the traces of the tools they call. We ask what such evaluations hide. Third, no detector trained on AgentDrift ranks AgentDojo traces better than chance (AUROC 0.47 to 0.52), including one that reaches 0.996 within AgentDojo. | **[Evidence-Guided Recovery for AI Agents with Ambiguous Tool Outcomes and Partial Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41453v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool timeouts can leave AI agents facing ambiguous tool outcomes and partial execution: an action may have failed, committed or remained in flight while other workflow steps have already completed. We formulate evidence-guided task recovery over approved obligations, provider capabilities and states compatible with public evidence. A shared admissibility check handles key expiry, authorization timing, dependencies and residual effects; a terminal certificate separates actual goal satisfaction from publicly supported safe completion. The known-model implementation combines standard observation-contingent planning with a separately implemented effect-history adjudicator. In a finite study of 37 assignments, planning and three active controls each certify 28 completions; four saved synthetic fee units incur additional computation. A preregistered assignment-held-out comparison then evaluates planning advice against evidence prompting with two local model bundles. Across 58 paired units in 19 previously developed task families, advice certifies 30 completions versus 9, a 36.2-percentage-point difference (exact family sign-flip p=0.0078), using 272 proposals versus 399. All 116 trajectories pass independent replay. Both models exhibit a positive completion difference. The comparison measures the evaluated advice interface under a common shield; search-versus-rule advice requires a separate matched comparison. Unknown and handoff outcomes remain explicit, with guarantees tied to the declared provider capabilities rather than arbitrary third-party exactly-once execution.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 1 figure, and 10 tables. Includes a finite recovery model, model-facing confirmation study with two local-model bundles, independent replay, and an explicit provider-capability scope</p></details> |
| **[EvoTeam: Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39813v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on collaboration among specialized agents to solve complex tasks. Existing methods improve MAS by refining the prompts, skills, and execution strategies of individual agents based on task feedback. However, improving individual agents does not necessarily lead to better team collaboration. Recent studies have therefore extended evolution to the collaboration level, using collective execution experience to adjust team organization, and inter-agent interactions. Nevertheless, evolving collaboration across heterogeneous tasks remains challenging, as existing approaches often constrain team reorganization within a shared structure and reuse interaction rules across incompatible team contexts. In this paper, we propose EvoTeam, a collaboration-centric evolution framework that jointly evolves team organization, interaction rules, and individual agent skills through execution experience. It maintains a library of reusable teams, selecting or creating agent teams and guiding collaboration through team-specific interaction rules. Collaboration experience accumulates within compatible team contexts, while individual skills remain reusable across teams. Experiments across four benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/DEEP-JLU/EvoTeam.</p></details> |  |
| **[BlindToChange: AI Agents Can Find Changes When Asked But Do They Look When Needed?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41435v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given that AI agents can search the web broadly and deeply in real time, it is tempting to conclude that their outputs account for recent world events. We show that this conclusion does not hold in practice. While agents can search and easily find recent changes, the correctness of their responses depends on whether they search and what they search for. Popular agentic search benchmarks like BrowseComp cannot expose failures wherein agents do not recognize their own assumptions that should be verified, since these benchmarks explicitly state what needs to be found. In contrast, many user requests state only the final information need (e.g., an itinerary for a trip), leaving the agent to discover relevant changes (e.g., the temporary closure of a museum) and account for their consequences. To highlight such gaps, we introduce BlindToChange, a benchmark that measures whether agents proactively discover recent events that affect their responses. Our benchmark comprises requests for recommendations where an obvious candidate is no longer applicable due to a recent real-world event. These events are drawn automatically from Wikidata, allowing BlindToChange to be periodically refreshed. Strikingly, we find a big gap -- as high as 27.5 points, even at high reasoning effort -- in systems' performance, when the recent event is not explicitly mentioned compared to when we ask about it directly. Analyzing search trajectories, we learn that more than half of the failures can be attributed to the fact that agents do not directly search to verify whether a relevant fact has changed. Our benchmark highlights, and tracks, a new axis of progress, wherein systems account for recent information that is not explicitly mentioned in the user request.</p></details> | Preprint |
| **[What Does a Repair Agent Gain from Remembering Its Failure? An Exploratory Study on Real Python Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41433v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When a coding agent fails a repair task, it is often retried with a record of the failure: the transcript, a self-written lesson, or extracted test signals. Two predeclared follow-ups repeated the plain-retry, transcript and lesson conditions with the larger gpt-5.4 and added a notice-only control. On the bug-revealing tests, every memory condition gained over a plain retry in point estimate (+5.5 to +9.9 points over 29.8%), but none significantly. Five further findings stand out. First, a model-written lesson showed no advantage over the transcript it summarises (-1.7 points, 95% CI [-8.7, +4.9]; -3.5 for gpt-5.4), although it costs an extra model call. Second, the transcript may do more than tell the agent that it failed: a notice alone produced no significant change, while the transcript resolved 12.2 points more failures than the notice for gpt-5.4-mini (CI [+4.2, +20.0], partial run of 156 failures; the only significant one of twelve predeclared contrasts, and not significant for gpt-5.4). Third, post hoc, fewer retries with memory ended without modifying any Python file, while the share of editing retries that touched a file the fix changes was near ceiling. Fourth, in a post hoc full-test-suite check, retries that had also seen the previous attempt's test results more often failed other tests that the reference fix passes, which shrinks their apparent gains. Fifth, outcomes varied far more between failures than between conditions, and we give the sample sizes a confirmatory study would need.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 3 figures, 8 tables. Our designed AX tools return compact observations, execute actions in batches, and report their effects as AX diffs, so the model needs fewer round trips. On OSWorld 2.0, HCU achieves the highest Partial and Binary scores among the compared systems. Analyzing agent trajectories, we find that all evaluated models improve with HCU yet allocate work across the channels differently and consistently outperform compared baselines in terms of both efficiency and task success, highlighting the value of unified computer-use interfaces that allow flexible channel selection and combination.</p></details> |  |
| **[VEvo-Harness: Causally-Guarded Self-Evolving Harness for Agentic Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39158v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The central bottleneck in visual generation and editing has migrated from achieving pixel-level fidelity to satisfying densely constrained user specifications through increasingly agentic workflows. With memorize and govern periods left open, they degrade into ungoverned iterators of repeated trial-and-error rather than continual self-evolving harness. Towards this issue, we present \textbf{VEvo-Harness}, the first causally-guarded continual self-evolving harness system for agentic visual generation. VEvo-Harness introduces four coupled innovations: (1) \textit{a visual traceback} that densifies sparse judgments into root-cause attributions with a hierarchical verification tree; (2) \textit{a visual continual governance} that admits only causally valid skill patches through counterfactual dual-arm image generation replay; (3) \textit{a lifecycle-managed harness state} that hierarchically distills root-cause-anchored episodes across working, experience, and pattern tiers into a persistent skill library of routable and causally verified capabilities; (4) \textit{a heterogeneous sandbox policy} that orchestrates a diverse action space of repair primitives, dynamically routing cost-aware interventions based on root causes to select the most effective strategy. | **[EvoMemBench: Benchmarking Agent Memory from a Self-Evolving Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18421v3)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent benchmarks for Large Language Model (LLM) agents mainly evaluate reasoning, planning, and execution. An independent checker accepts every retained trace and pair and rejects 26/26 semantic regressions; coverage and normalizer suites reject 24/24 and 720/720 mutants; and 168/168 commit probes match their declared outcomes. | **[Anchor and Perturb: Lazy Agent Remediation by Exploration Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27365v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Anchor and Perturb (AnP) is a lightweight framework that resolves multi-agent coordination failures by decoupling exploratory variance injection from recurrent manifold stability. Specifically, AnP isolates underperforming lazy agents and injects an asymmetric exploratory pulse into targeted coordinates whilst anchoring converged teammates to nominal greedy exploitation. | **[PriorityFEC: Deadline-Aware Streaming FEC for KV-Cache Handoff in Edge Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21390v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems are increasingly deployed at the edge to support complex tasks that require collaboration and information exchange among multiple agents. However, when task contexts are passed across agents, shared context that has already been processed by upstream agents often needs to be prefilled again, resulting in substantial redundant computation. Under strict time-to-first-token (TTFT) deadlines and unreliable edge links, delivering the complete KV cache may be infeasible, making it necessary to prioritize and protect the most valuable KV information within a limited transmission budget. This paper proposes PriorityFEC, a utility-aware KV-cache handoff scheme that jointly exploits model-side KV importance and transport-side reliability. PriorityFEC first constructs a utility-aware source stream by placing high-utility KV blocks at earlier transmission positions. It then protects the ordered stream using a feedback-adaptive Streaming Forward Erasure Correction (FEC) mechanism, which schedules repair packets according to receiver feedback before the TTFT deadline. We implement PriorityFEC on a distributed edge LLM serving prototype and evaluate it with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct across multiple datasets. Results show that PriorityFEC achieves superior task quality over the compared transport schemes, with its advantages becoming particularly pronounced under stringent deadline constraints. These results demonstrate the importance of jointly optimizing KV-cache utility and deadline-aware transport reliability when reusable LLM state must be handed off across unreliable edge networks.</p></details> |  |

Compared with the Fortran/OpenACC baseline on GH200, the port achieved application speedups of $1.71\times$ on GH200 and $1.54\times$ on MI300A. | **[ART of PIV: Agentic Real-Time Particle Image Velocimetry for Active Flow Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41304v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Particle image velocimetry (PIV) measures fluid motion from images of tracer particles, but its accuracy depends on choices throughout the estimation pipeline, including image preprocessing, flow estimation, and postprocessing. These choices are typically left to expert tuning, and a fixed configuration can become less accurate as conditions change. This matters especially when PIV supplies feedback for real-time control, where measurements must be both accurate and fast, and flow conditions vary. We introduce ART of PIV -- short for Agentic Real-Time and also a nod to the art of tuning a PIV pipeline -- which automates pipeline choice for each image pair. A learned agent selects a configuration according to a chosen measurement objective from a library assembled for a specified speed budget. Across cross-correlation, classical optical-flow, and learning-based estimators, ART of PIV reduces average end-point error (AEPE) by 12.2-22.4 % relative to the best single estimator at matched speed, with gains extending to 1000-4000 image pair/s. In a simulated local velocity regulation task, it reduces mean normalized closed-loop cost by 13.24-13.59 percentage points relative to a tuned fixed estimator, with the same classical controller in both cases. In a tabletop water channel, we illustrate two ways to tune ART of PIV for a fluidic pinball of three rotating cylinders: selecting between complementary optical-flow tunings for accuracy, and selecting regions of interest with coherent rotation or shear using a common flow estimator. | **[In-Model Context Compression with Agentic Soft Tokens for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41291v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon LLM agents play an important role in solving complex and sustained tasks, with ever-growing interaction contexts. Specifically, ASTC uses the model's native recurrent dynamics to determine the already absorbed information, leveraging the commonly-used hybrid Gated Delta Network (GDN)-attention. It then carries this information forward as agentic soft tokens while retaining exact values as hard tokens. Extensive experiments on long-horizon tasks show that ASTC matches or exceeds the uncompressed agent's accuracy with up to 50.68% fewer end-to-end Attention Pairs, while trained compressors require 15.4 to 103.7 times more Attention Pairs per successful task. | **[Truthful and Admissible Principal--Agent Testing with Nuisance Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41283v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Statistical contracts reward experimenters according to the evidence produced by a costly study, so the reward rule influences both who participates and what they report. Principal--agent testing theory explains how to prevent ineffective proposals from being profitable. We ask when such contracts can also elicit honest parameter reports if the decision concerns a mean effect but the variance or distributional shape is unknown. Under risk neutrality and a fixed participation cost, we construct menus of e-values --- nonnegative normalized rewards with expected value at most one under every null --- such that (i) agents who know they are null have no incentive to participate, (ii) every alternative type earns positive expected profit and is thus incentivized to participate, (iii) truthful reporting uniquely maximizes expected their reward, (iv) and each contract is admissible: its payouts cannot be pointwise improved while retaining null validity. For Gaussian observations, we treat separately agents who know both mean and variance and agents who know only their mean; in the latter case, truthfulness holds at every variance without requiring a nuisance prior. Multi-sample extensions preserve these incentives and can reward accumulating evidence. Beyond Gaussian models, we show that two independent nonnegative observations suffice to elicit any finite mean above a positive null threshold, without any further distributional knowledge, but one observation cannot combine strict mean elicitation with individual admissibility on this class. Finally, we distinguish the existence of useful tests from the identification needed for truthful contracts. Boundedness is not required, and finitely many null constraints do not by themselves guarantee admissible elicitation.</p></details> | 30 pages |

We argue that, to define these behavioral transitions, the missing ingredient is $\textbf{events}$. Events are changes in the state of the system that induce qualitative changes in the task. Based on this view, we introduce a framework that decouples agent identity from behavior, capturing a continuous manifold from which agents instantiate their behaviors in response to events. This framework is based on two elements. First, to build an expressive behavior manifold, we introduce Neural Manifold Diversity (NMD), a formal distance metric that remains well-defined when behaviors are transient and agent-agnostic. Second, we use an event-based hypernetwork that generates Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) modules over a shared team policy, enabling on-the-fly agent-policy reconfiguration in response to events. We prove that this construction ensures that diversity does not interfere with reward maximization by design. Empirical results demonstrate that our framework outperforms established baselines across benchmarks while exhibiting zero-shot generalization, and being the only method that solves tasks requiring sequential behavior reassignment.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Elastic Least Privilege for Agent Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40981v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General-purpose AI agents require authority that changes between tasks and during execution. Common deployments trade broad permissions that expose unnecessary authority and narrow permissions that raise approval requests. Existing mechanisms can enforce dynamic authority, but agent tool interfaces rarely provide the agent's planner, the component that chooses its actions, a means of comparing the authority required by alternative actions or of seeing the cost of the authority it already holds. Drawing on the rich history of access control research, we propose the concept of \emph{elastic least privilege}, in which an agent begins with a small baseline of authority and may acquire more within an owner-defined envelope. Elevations are leased and measured against a replenishing budget. Their cost increases with power, proximity to mandatory safety invariants, and repeated requests. Costs are visible to the planner, which can prefer lower-privilege choices and release authority it no longer needs. Authority returns automatically to the baseline when a lease expires, when it is released, or when the budget is exhausted, while persistent legitimate demand may prompt an owner-approved change to the baseline. Building on prior risk-priced and budget-aware access control, we redesign these mechanisms for agents. How best to implement the approach and how well it works in practice are open research questions. In this paper, we present how the approach could work, illustrate the model, and set an open research agenda.</p></details> |  |

| **[Does Showing Agents the Privilege of Their Tools Reduce Over-Privileged Tool Selection?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40975v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When several tools can complete a task, an agent that picks the most powerful one holds more authority than the task needs. Combined with the instruction it more often avoids escalation, and the share of scenarios completed without a risk tool rises on seven of the eight models, reaching 51.1\% safe completion from a baseline of 18.8\% on the strongest-performing configuration. | **[Renegotiable Options Fail to Prevent Hold-Up Between Two Profit-Maximizing Frontier LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40961v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Do contract terms that protect investment between people also protect it between profit-maximizing language-model agents? Once that cost is sunk, the buyer can offer almost nothing and the seller will still accept, so a seller who foresees this does not adapt. One remedy is an option: the buyer may buy at a fixed price. The agents did what game theory predicts: under the renegotiable option no seller adapted, and under the option that rules out this renegotiation every seller did. In a second experiment in which sellers no longer stated their expected revenue before deciding, removing the profit instruction left investment unchanged under no contract and both options. When we changed the numbers so that the buyer would exercise the option even without adaptation, adapting only added cost, and no seller adapted: the agents did not simply adapt whenever an option was offered or whenever its price covered the cost. | **[Benchmarking Forecasting Agents on Yesterday's Tomorrows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40940v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Digital agents can now gather and organize information, reason over it, and use tools to complete complex tasks. | **[Agentic World Modeling for Agent Test-time Scaling without Environment Replica](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40927v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Test-time scaling (TTS) improves large language model agents by sampling and selecting among multiple candidate trajectories, but common realizations assume that these trajectories can be executed in parallel. | **[Towards Portable Specification of the Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40917v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models act only through the infrastructure that surrounds them. Existing model cards and agent cards provide release and operational information, but do not establish the sequence of effective configurations within a run. Harnesses can change between releases and between runs, and, where agents edit their own tools and configuration, within a run. Thus, TSF researchers must constantly adapt or implement existing works. However, independent implementations can differ in experimental settings and are difficult to maintain. In reproduction and benchmarking, TSFLab improves task completion by 20-163% and cuts fresh tokens per completed task by 11-66% across baselines. | **[From GPU to TPU with Coding Agents: What Works, What Fails, and What It Costs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34422v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A coding agent can migrate a machine learning workload, report success, and still change how the model trains. Of 70 execution attempts, 53 fail (76%), and 26 of these failures (49%) raise no error. | **[STRIATUM-CTF: A Protocol-Driven Agentic Framework for Autonomous CTF Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22577v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous cyber operations increasingly depend on agents that can chain reconnaissance, exploitation, and analysis across many tools while maintaining state over long task horizons.
